# Exploring the Fundamental Concepts of Machine Learning for Medical Enhancement

  
  
**Rohit Bathla^([1](#9789815305128-C14.html#aff1))****,** **Prateek Jain^([1](#9789815305128-C14.html#aff1), [\*](#9789815305128-C14.html#cor1))****,** **Rachna Behl^([1](#9789815305128-C14.html#aff1))****,** **Abhishek Saxena^([2](#9789815305128-C14.html#aff2))**  
¹ Manav Rachna International Institute of Research & Studies, Faridabad, Haryana, India  
² Manav Rachna University, Faridabad, Haryana, India  
  

## Abstract

Machine learning (ML), a subset of artificial intelligence (AI), has recently gained prominence in the technology domain and is driving advancements in the healthcare system. This innovation enables healthcare professionals to prioritize patient diagnosis over time-consuming and intricate treatment procedures, significantly transforming the healthcare sector. Considering the challenges posed by shortages and high demand for skilled practitioners in healthcare systems, the emergence of machine learning presents a promising solution. Consequently, it offers hope for countries grappling with overburdened healthcare systems and a shortage of healthcare professionals. Utilising healthcare data can provide valuable insights, such as pinpointing ideal trial samples, gathering extra data points, continually analysing data from trial participants, and minimising data-related errors. Employing a machine learning-based approach aids in detecting early symptoms of an epidemic or pandemic, allowing more time to focus on patient health and care rather than data entry or information retrieval. This chapter examines the prospects and scope of Machine Learning in healthcare. The key Machine Learning applications for healthcare are identified and discussed. The ML-based solutions are utilised to lower overall healthcare expenses, improve the general efficacy of hospitals and healthcare systems, and provide a variety of treatment alternatives. Machine learning will soon influence hospitals and doctors.

**Keywords:** Artificial intelligence, Blockchain, Healthcare, Medicine, Machine learning.  
  

* * *

^(\*) **Corresponding author Prateek Jain:** Manav Rachna International Institute of Research & Studies, Faridabad, Haryana, India; E-mail: [prateekjain2010@gmail.com](mailto:prateekjain2010@gmail.com)  
  

## INTRODUCTION

### Overview of Machine Learning

The field of machine learning (ML) in healthcare is developing quickly and uses advanced computational methods to evaluate and understand complicated medical

data. It has much potential to enhance diagnostic precision, optimize patient outcomes, and streamline healthcare procedures.

The pharmaceutical business, which has traditionally been reluctant to adopt new technologies, is now experiencing an enormous change.

The sector is concentrating on emerging technologies, including machine learning (ML), blockchain, Artificial Intelligence (AI/ML), and the Internet of Things (IoT). The introduction of new technologies brings with it issues for organizations.

Combined with outside factors like weather and pollution exposure, machine learning finds a wide range of applications in health maintenance, feeding on case management of common ailments to enhance patient health data. Large datasets can be broken down using ML approaches, which can assist healthcare practitioners in producing medical solutions that are precise and customized to each patient's unique needs. Because machine learning can sift through millions of data points and find potentially hazardous ones, it is being utilized increasingly to detect dangers. Fig. ([**1**](#9789815305128-C14.html#f1)) displays the interrelated fields with machine learning.

![](Images/9789815305128-C14-F1.jpg)**Fig. (1))**  
Interrelated fields of machine learning. 

Within AI, it is a subset. Without explicit modelling, machine learning models pick up knowledge from experiences as people do. These machine learning models adapt, evolve, learn, and grow independently when subjected to new data. Put differently, machine learning entails the discovery of useful information covertly. The chosen algorithm is trained as part of the machine-learning process. The final machine learning algorithm might be created using known or unknown training data. Subsequently, the ML algorithm is applied to the new data to see if it functions properly. The next stage is to compare the prediction and results with each other to see if the ML algorithm performed as intended. The ML algorithm is trained repeatedly until the data scientist receives the proper output if the outcomes and the prediction do not match. This makes it easier for the machine learning algorithm to train continually and can generate a very good response with steadily improving accuracy over time. The ML life cycle process is depicted in the following picture, which explains how subject matter and business stakeholders are involved at various stages. Understanding that the ML lifespan is a participatory process is crucial. In the healthcare industry, machine learning is used to extract knowledge from massive medical datasets to improve patient outcomes, streamline daily tasks for healthcare workers, expedite medical research, and boost operational effectiveness.

#### Ranking

It can facilitate the search by prioritizing the relevant information.

#### Prediction

It can forecast the distribution of future events based on historical patterns and available data.

#### Classification

ML algorithms can assist in classifying and regulating the type of illness or medical condition a patient is coping with.

#### Automation

It can handle routine, repetitive operations like inventory management, data entry, appointment scheduling, *etc*., that require more time and effort from physicians and patients.

#### Suggestions

ML systems can offer crucial medical information without requiring ardent search efforts.

#### Anomaly Detection

This technique is used in the healthcare industry to identify recurring patterns and determine whether further action is necessary.

#### Clustering

To identify trends and guide future research, putting similar medical cases in one category can be useful.

### Importance of Machine Learning

The exponential increase in the volume and diversity of data, the heightened accessibility and affordability of computational power, and the ubiquitous availability of high-speed Internet propel the importance of machine learning. These elements of digital transformation facilitate the swift and automated creation of models capable of effectively and precisely analyzing vast and complex datasets.

It presents numerous possibilities for lowering costs, minimizing risks, and enhancing the overall quality of life. These possibilities encompass suggesting products/services, identifying cybersecurity threats, and aiding the adoption of autonomous vehicles. As data availability grows and computational capabilities improve, machine learning is increasingly becoming a fundamental component of everyday life, ready to integrate seamlessly into various facets of human existence.

### Scope of Machine Learning

In the last decade, there have been notable and rapid progressions in the field of machine learning (ML) applied to healthcare. Extensive efforts have been dedicated to addressing diverse healthcare needs, presenting opportunities to improve patient care, alleviate the workload on healthcare professionals, streamline healthcare processes, and empower individuals. Particularly noteworthy is the considerable success achieved by ML in healthcare, especially in the domain of computer vision, evident in the development of image-based triage and secondary reader systems.

## SURVEY FOR MACHINE LEARNING IN HEALTHCARE

The proposed work of Dhillon and Singh \[[1](#9789815305128-C14.html#r1)\] explains several kinds of machine learning algorithms, including supervised, unsupervised, and reinforcement learning, which are employed in machine learning. The application of machine learning techniques to analyses diverse medical data is then reviewed. Data of all kinds, including sensors, Omics, and clinical data, are used in healthcare. Electronic health records, which keep patient data gathered throughout continuing therapy, are included in clinical data. It takes a lot of work to handle this raw data manually. Machine learning has become a vital tool for data analysis. Machine learning makes more accurate predictions about healthcare data by utilizing sophisticated algorithms and various statistical methodologies.

In this survey, Rahman and Zhou \[[2](#9789815305128-C14.html#r2)\] examined several machine-learning techniques for use in medical settings. Following a thorough introduction and analysis of supervised and unsupervised machine learning methods, they also showed how to do time series tasks using historical data. They evaluated their viability for both small and large datasets.

Deo \[[3](#9789815305128-C14.html#r3)\] focuses on how computers learn from data. It emerges from the nexus of computer science, focusing on effective computing techniques and statistics, which aims to infer relationships from data. The special computational difficulties in creating statistical models from enormous datasets that might contain billions or trillions of data points motivate this union of mathematics and computer science. It is helpful to divide the different forms of learning that computers utilize into groups like supervised and unsupervised learning.

To provide readers with the fundamental knowledge required to comprehend articles on the subject, we want to present the core concepts of machine learning to this community in this paper \[[4](#9789815305128-C14.html#r4)\]. We aim to direct readers to pertinent research and provide context for machine learning applications in the domains of pharmacometrics, clinical pharmacology, and molecular biology, even though we will not delve into the specifics and theoretical underpinnings.

In a study \[[5](#9789815305128-C14.html#r5)\], the author explored deep learning in computer vision, natural language processing, reinforcement learning, and generalized approaches, focusing on deep learning techniques for the healthcare industry. We discuss the potential applications of these computational methods in several important medical domains and investigate the development of end-to-end systems. We describe the application of natural language processing to areas such as electronic health record data, and our discussion of computer vision is mostly focused on medical imaging. Similarly, generalized deep-learning techniques for genomics are examined, and reinforcement learning about robotically assisted surgery is explored.

In this work \[[6](#9789815305128-C14.html#r6)\], we outline the problems related to the many healthcare application domains that use these techniques from a security and privacy perspective. Furthermore, we outline several approaches to guarantee safe and privacy-preserving ML for medical applications. In conclusion, we understand the present research obstacles and promising avenues for future investigations.

This survey \[[7](#9789815305128-C14.html#r7)\] offers a comprehensive overview of RL applications in various healthcare domains, including automated medical diagnosis, dynamic treatment regimes in chronic diseases and critical care, and many other control or scheduling problems that have permeated every part of the healthcare system. It begins by briefly reviewing RL research's theoretical underpinnings and key methods. Furthermore, we highlight possible solutions and future research paths and discuss the current study's difficulties and unresolved problems.

Since models can potentially exacerbate preexisting health disparities, machine learning (ML) in healthcare presents several ethical issues. This article \[[8](#9789815305128-C14.html#r8)\] discusses moral issues surrounding egalitarian machine learning for healthcare advancement. We use social justice to contextualize the ethics of machine learning in healthcare. From problem selection to post-deployment concerns, we detail obstacles in a suggested pipeline of ethical machine learning in the health sector and describe the ongoing work in that direction. We conclude with a summary of suggestions for resolving these issues.

This study \[[9](#9789815305128-C14.html#r9)\] examines machine learning (ML) and its application in healthcare before going into related aspects and suitable ML pillars for healthcare architecture. Lastly, it highlighted and reviewed the important uses of ML in healthcare. Using this technology in healthcare operations could have a significant positive impact on the company. Hospitals and healthcare systems can offer individualized and varied treatment options, save healthcare costs, and become more efficient by implementing machine learning (ML)-based technology. ML will soon impact both physicians and hospitals. It will be crucial for developing clinical decision support, illness diagnosis, and individualized treatment regimens to get the greatest outcomes.

The author will discuss \[[10](#9789815305128-C14.html#r10)\] the potential benefits for people of using ML/AI in healthcare communication in this subject overview. This covers chatbots for medical imaging, cancer treatment, and COVID-19 health education.

This chapter \[[11](#9789815305128-C14.html#r11)\] examines the most recent research on using machine learning to advance healthcare solutions. But we also discuss machine learning technology's constraints, difficulties, and possibilities in the healthcare industry. Machine learning applications can be used for diagnosis, prognosis, and creation of the ideal treatment plan for the identified condition to track and evaluate treatment efficacy in the healthcare industry. Medical professionals can benefit from machine learning technology by accessing quicker and more precise solutions. Readers will find the principles and the latest advancements in the cutting-edge machine learning-based healthcare system in this chapter. But as medical knowledge and technology advance, they also produce novel situations that must be examined from an interdisciplinary and comprehensive perspective.

Author \[[12](#9789815305128-C14.html#r12)\] examines different industry projects utilizing machine learning in the healthcare sector and discusses the possible applications of these technologies.

Genetic data, medical document natural language processing, and medical imaging are the primary domains where machine learning is used. Numerous of these fields concentrate on the prediction, detection, and diagnosis of diseases. There is currently a sizable medical device infrastructure that creates data, but frequently there needs to be a supporting infrastructure to make good use of such data. Medical information can be found in various formats, which might lead to noise and formatting issues. We look at a quick overview of machine learning's history, some fundamentals about the methods, and the condition of the technology in the healthcare industry today \[[13](#9789815305128-C14.html#r13)\].

## APPLICATIONS OF MACHINE LEARNING IN HEALTHCARE

Machine learning (ML) has a wide range of applications in healthcare, transforming various aspects of the industry to improve patient outcomes, optimize processes, and enhance decision-making \[[14](#9789815305128-C14.html#r14)\]. Here are some notable applications of machine learning in healthcare:

### Medical Imaging Analysis

Medical imaging data, including X-rays, MRIs, and CT scans, can be analyzed by ML algorithms to help diagnose conditions including cancer, tumors, and neurological problems.

### Diagnostic Decision Support Systems

ML models can help clinicians by providing insights into the likelihood of specific diseases based on patient data, symptoms, and medical history.

### Drug Response Prediction

ML models analyze patient data to predict how individuals might respond to specific medications, allowing for the customization of treatment plans.

### Precision Medicine

Tailoring treatment strategies based on individual patient's genetic makeup, lifestyle, and specific characteristics.

### Early Warning Systems

ML can be used to identify early signs of deterioration in patients' health, enabling timely intervention and preventing complications.

### Optimizing Treatment Protocols

ML algorithms assist healthcare providers in choosing the most effective treatment options based on historical data and patient characteristics.

### Remote Patient Monitoring

ML algorithms analyse data from wearable devices and other remote monitoring tools to track patients' health status and provide real-time feedback.

### Predictive Analytics for Chronic Diseases

ML can predict the likelihood of exacerbations in chronic conditions, allowing for proactive management.

### Claims and Billing Fraud Detection

ML models can identify patterns indicative of fraudulent activities in healthcare claims, helping prevent financial losses.

### Revenue Cycle Optimization

ML assists in streamlining administrative processes, reducing billing errors, and improving overall revenue cycle management.

### Electronic Health Record (EHR) Analysis

NLP algorithms extract valuable information from unstructured clinical notes, making assessment and use of patient data easier for healthcare providers.

### Clinical Coding Automation

ML-powered NLP tools help automate the coding of medical procedures and diagnoses, reducing manual workload.

### Epidemiological Modeling

ML algorithms analyse large datasets to predict and monitor the spread of infectious diseases, facilitating early intervention and resource allocation.

### Disease Surveillance

ML assists in monitoring and tracking public health trends, enabling a proactive response to emerging health threats.

### Genomic Data Analysis

ML is used to analyse vast genomic datasets, identify genetic markers associated with diseases, and support genomics research.

### Drug Discovery

ML accelerates drug discovery by predicting potential drug candidates, optimizing molecular structures, and identifying potential targets.

### Automated Tumor Detection

ML algorithms assist radiologists in detecting and characterizing tumors in medical images, improving accuracy and efficiency.

### Histopathology Image Analysis

ML aids pathologists in analyzing pathology slides, identifying patterns, and supporting diagnostic decisions.

### Predictive Analytics for Mental Health

ML models can analyze behavioral and clinical data to predict and intervene in mental health crises.

### Virtual Mental Health Assistants

ML-powered chatbots and virtual assistants provide mental health support and resources.

With the potential to improve diagnosis, treatment, and the delivery of healthcare, these applications show the adaptability and power of machine learning in the medical field. For machine learning to be used in healthcare settings responsibly and effectively, however, issues including data protection, regulatory compliance, and ethical considerations must be addressed \[[15](#9789815305128-C14.html#r15)\].

## MACHINE LEARNING CHALLENGES AND CONSIDERATIONS

Machine learning (ML) application in healthcare has enormous potential to enhance patient care overall, including diagnosis and treatment strategies. However, it also raises several issues and concerns that must be properly considered. A multidisciplinary approach based on the cooperation between data scientists, healthcare practitioners, ethicists, legislators, and technology specialists is needed to address these issues. To guarantee the appropriate and efficient application of machine learning in healthcare, balancing innovation and ethical and regulatory concerns is imperative. The following are some of the major issues and factors to consider \[[16](#9789815305128-C14.html#r16), [17](#9789815305128-C14.html#r17)\] (Table **[1](#9789815305128-C14.html#t1)**).

Table 1 **Challenges and considerations of ML.**  

| \- || **Challenges** || **Considerations** |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Data Privacy and Security** || Sensitive healthcare data must adhere to stringent privacy laws. Maintaining patient confidentiality while using data for training ML models is a significant challenge. || To secure patient data, implementing strong encryption, anonymization, and access control mechanisms. HIPAA (Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act) compliance is essential. |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Data Quality and Availability** || ML models heavily rely on high-quality, diverse, and representative datasets. In healthcare, obtaining such data can be challenging due to issues like data silos, incomplete records, and variations in data formats. || Investing in data cleaning, standardization, and integration processes. Collaborating across institutions to create larger and more diverse datasets. |
| **Interoperability** || Healthcare systems often use different standards and formats, leading to interoperability issues. ML models may struggle to integrate with various data sources and systems. || Promoting the adoption of standardized formats and interfaces in healthcare IT systems. Developing APIs (Application Programming Interfaces) that enable seamless data exchange. |
| **Regulatory Compliance** || Healthcare is highly regulated, and ML applications may need to undergo rigorous approval processes. Navigating these regulatory frameworks can be time-consuming and complex. || Collaborating with regulatory bodies to establish guidelines for the ethical and safe use of ML in healthcare. Ensuring transparency in ML algorithms to facilitate regulatory review. |
| **Ethical Considerations** || ML models may inadvertently perpetuate biases present in training data. Ensuring fairness and avoiding discrimination is a critical ethical consideration. || Regularly auditing and evaluating models for biases. Incorporating ethical considerations into the design and deployment of ML systems. Striving for diversity and inclusivity in dataset curation. |
| **Clinical Interpretability** || Many ML models, especially complex ones like deep neural networks, are often considered “black boxes,” making it challenging for clinicians to understand their decisions. || Developing interpretable ML models or methods to provide explanations for model predictions. Ensuring that clinicians can trust and understand the outputs of ML systems. |
| **Integration into Clinical Workflow** || Implementing ML solutions seamlessly into existing clinical workflows can be challenging. Resistance to change and lack of user-friendly interfaces can hinder adoption. || Involving healthcare professionals in the design and implementation process. Developing user-friendly interfaces and providing training to healthcare staff on the use of ML tools. |
| **Resource Constraints** || Healthcare organizations may have limited resources, both in terms of finances and skilled personnel, to implement and maintain ML solutions. || Consideration: Exploring collaborative efforts, sharing resources, and considering scalable and cost-effective ML solutions. Investing in training programs to build a workforce skilled in both healthcare and machine learning. |
| **Patient Trust and Engagement** || Patients may be skeptical or uneasy about the use of ML in their healthcare. Building and maintaining trust is crucial for successful implementation. || Educating patients about the benefits and limitations of ML in healthcare. Involving patients in decision-making processes and ensuring transparency about how their data is used. |
| **Long-term Monitoring and Maintenance** || ML models may degrade in performance over time due to changes in data distribution or evolving clinical practices. || Implementing continuous monitoring systems and regular model updates. Establishing protocols for model retraining to ensure ongoing effectiveness. |

  

## BENEFITS OF MACHINE LEARNING IN HEALTHCARE

Machine learning (ML) in healthcare offers many benefits that can significantly improve patient outcomes, enhance efficiency, and advance medical research. Here are some key benefits of integrating machine learning into healthcare \[[18](#9789815305128-C14.html#r18)^(\-)[20](#9789815305128-C14.html#r20)\].

![](Images/9789815305128-C14-F2.jpg)**Fig. (2))**  
Role of machine learning in health care. 

### Improved Diagnostic Accuracy

ML algorithms can analyse complex medical data, including images and test results, to assist healthcare professionals in diagnosing diseases with higher accuracy and speed. Early detection of diseases, such as cancer, allows for prompt intervention and improved treatment outcomes.

### Personalised Treatment Plans

ML enables the analysis of individual patient data to tailor treatment plans based on genetic factors, medical history, and other personalised parameters. Personalised medicine enhances treatment efficacy, reduces side effects, and improves patient compliance.

### Efficient Data Analysis

ML algorithms can process and analyse large datasets at a scale and speed beyond human capabilities. Rapid analysis of electronic health records (EHRs) and medical imaging data accelerates research and improves decision-making.

### Clinical Decision Support Systems (CDSS)

ML-driven CDSS assists healthcare professionals by providing real-time insights and recommendations for diagnosis and treatment. CDSS enhances decision-making by considering a broad range of patient data and medical knowledge.

### Predictive Analytics for Preventive Care

ML models can predict patient risks and identify potential health issues before they become severe. Early intervention based on predictive analytics can lead to better preventive care and reduce the burden on healthcare systems.

### Remote Patient Monitoring

ML applications in wearable devices and remote monitoring tools enable continuous tracking of patients' health outside traditional healthcare settings. Remote monitoring enhances patient engagement, allows for timely interventions, and reduces hospital readmissions.

### Optimized Resource Allocation

ML helps optimise resource allocation in healthcare facilities by predicting patient admission rates, optimising staff schedules, and managing inventory efficiently. Resource optimisation leads to cost savings and improved overall healthcare service delivery.

### Automated Administrative Processes

ML streamlines administrative tasks like appointment scheduling, billing, and claims processing. Automation of routine administrative processes reduces errors and allows healthcare professionals to focus on patient care.

### Drug Discovery and Development

ML accelerates drug discovery by analysing large datasets, predicting potential drug candidates, and optimising molecular structures. Reduced time and cost in drug development lead to faster access to new treatments.

### Enhanced Imaging Analysis

ML algorithms improve the analysis of medical imaging data, such as MRI and CT scans, aiding in detecting abnormalities and assisting radiologists in diagnosis. Automated image analysis increases efficiency and reduces the likelihood of human error.

### Epidemiological Surveillance and Outbreak Prediction

ML models analyse vast amounts of data for epidemiological surveillance, enabling the prediction and monitoring of disease outbreaks. Early detection of outbreaks allows for proactive public health measures and resource allocation.

### Continuous Learning and Adaptation

ML models can continuously learn and adapt based on new data and evolving healthcare trends. Continuous learning improves the accuracy and relevance of ML applications over time.

### Patient Engagement and Education

ML-powered applications provide personalized health recommendations and educational resources to engage patients in managing their health. Increased patient engagement can lead to better adherence to treatment plans and lifestyle changes.

### Support for Medical Research

ML facilitates advanced data analysis in medical research, uncovering patterns and correlations in large datasets that may lead to discoveries. ML supports researchers in identifying potential risk factors, treatment responses, and biomarkers.

Healthcare greatly benefits from machine learning; but, to ensure responsible and secure application, data privacy, regulatory compliance, and ethical considerations must be addressed. Collaboration between healthcare professionals, data scientists, and other stakeholders is crucial for successfully integrating machine learning into healthcare workflows.

## MACHINE LEARNING ALGORITHMS USED IN HEALTHCARE

Machine learning (ML) algorithms play a crucial role in various applications within the healthcare sector. The choice of algorithm depends on the specific task or problem at hand. Here are some common machine learning algorithms used in healthcare \[[21](#9789815305128-C14.html#r21), [22](#9789815305128-C14.html#r22)\]:

### Supervised Learning Algorithms

A class of machine learning algorithms known as supervised learning algorithms uses labelled datasets where data is linked with appropriate output labels to train the model. To be able to predict or classify new, unknown data, the algorithm must be able to learn a mapping from input to output. Several popular supervised learning algorithms are as follows:

#### Logistic Regression

Predicting the likelihood that an observation will belong to one of two classes is the aim of the statistical technique known as logistic regression. Despite its name, logistic regression is not utilized for regression but for classification. In healthcare, logistic regression is commonly employed for disease prediction, risk assessment, and outcome modeling tasks.

##### Application

Disease prediction, outcome modelling.

##### Use Case

Predicting the likelihood of a patient developing a disease based on input features.

#### Support Vector Machines (SVM)

Strong supervised learning algorithms like Support Vector Machines (SVM) are employed for regression and classification problems. SVM is frequently used in the healthcare industry for medical image analysis, outcome prediction, and disease classification applications.

##### Application

Image classification, disease diagnosis.

##### Use Case

Classifying medical images (*e.g*., X-rays, MRI scans) to detect abnormalities or diseases.

#### Random Forest

An ensemble learning technique called Random Forest is applied to regression and classification problems. During training, it builds many decision trees. As a final prediction, it produces each tree's mean prediction (for regression) or the mode (for classification). In healthcare, Random Forest is often applied to disease prediction, risk assessment, and medical image analysis tasks.

##### Application

Disease risk prediction, diagnostic decision support.

##### Use Case

Predicting the risk of diseases and providing decision support for clinicians.

#### Gradient Boosting

An ensemble learning method called gradient boosting is applied to regression and classification problems. It builds a series of weak learners (usually decision trees) sequentially, with each new learner addressing the deficiencies of the combined model formed by the existing ones. Gradient Boosting is known for its high predictive accuracy and is commonly applied in healthcare for tasks such as disease prediction, risk assessment, and outcome modelling.

##### Application

Predictive modelling, personalized treatment plans.

##### Use Case

Developing models that predict patient responses to different treatments.

### Unsupervised Learning Algorithms:

Unsupervised learning algorithms uncover patterns or relationships in data without explicit instruction or labelled objectives. Unlike supervised learning, which uses labelled data to train the algorithm, unsupervised learning involves exploring the data's inherent structure. Common applications in healthcare include clustering similar patient groups or discovering patterns in medical images. Here are some key unsupervised learning algorithms:

#### K-Means Clustering

A well-liked unsupervised machine learning technique for splitting a dataset into K unique, non-overlapping subsets is called K-Means Clustering (clusters). Sorting related data points together and separating dissimilar ones is the aim. K-Means is widely applied in various domains, including healthcare, where it can be used for patient segmentation, grouping similar diseases, or analysing patterns in medical data.

##### Application

Patient stratification, cohort identification.

##### Use Case

Grouping patients based on similarities in their medical data for targeted interventions.

#### Hierarchical Clustering

Hierarchical Clustering is an unsupervised machine-learning algorithm that groups similar data points into nested clusters. It creates a hierarchical structure of clusters, often visualised as a tree-like diagram known as a dendrogram. Hierarchical Clustering is commonly used in healthcare for patient stratification, identifying disease subtypes, or organising medical data into meaningful groups.

##### Application

Disease classification, patient profiling.

##### Use Case

Clustering patients based on health characteristics for personalised healthcare plans.

#### Principal Component Analysis (PCA)

Principal component analysis, or PCA, is a popular dimensionality reduction method in data analysis and machine learning. As much of the original variability as feasible is preserved throughout transforming high-dimensional data into a lower-dimensional representation using PCA. PCA can be used in the medical field to lower the dimensionality of complicated information, including gene expression profiles or data from medical imaging.

##### Application

Dimensionality reduction in medical data.

##### Use Case

Reducing the number of features while retaining essential information in datasets like gene expression data.

### Reinforcement Learning Algorithms

Machine learning called reinforcement learning (RL), teaches an agent to make decisions by interacting with its surroundings. The agent aims to learn a policy that maximizes the cumulative reward over time. It gets feedback through rewards or penalties based on its behaviors. RL has applications in various domains, including robotics, game playing, and healthcare. Here are some key reinforcement learning algorithms (Table **[2](#9789815305128-C14.html#t2)**):

Table 2 **Types of Reinforcement Learning Algorithm.**  

| \- || **Type** || **Objective** || **Use Case** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Q-Learning** || Value-based || Learn the optimal action-value function representing the expected cumulative reward of acting. || Game playing, robotic control, optimization problems. |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Deep Q Network (DQN)** || Value-based || Extends Q-learning to handle high-dimensional state spaces by using a deep neural network. || Playing video games, robotic control with complex sensor inputs. |
| **Policy Gradient Methods** || Policy-based || Directly learn the policy, the probability distribution over actions given a state. || Continuous action spaces, robotic control, natural language processing. |
| **Actor-Critic** || Hybrid || Combines a policy network (actor) and a value network (critic) to improve learning stability. || Continuous action spaces, balancing exploration and exploitation. |
| **Proximal Policy Optimization** || Policy-based || Optimizes the policy in an iterative manner while ensuring that policy updates are not too large. || Robotic control, game playing |
| **Deep Deterministic Policy Gradients** || Policy-based || Extension of DQN to handle continuous action spaces by combining deep learning with actor-critic methods. || Robotic control, continuous control problems. |
| **Monte Carlo Methods** || Model-free || By averaging the observed returns, one can approximate the value of states or state-action pairings.  
Use Case: Game playing, episodic tasks. || Game playing, episodic tasks |
| **Temporal Difference (TD) Learning** || Model-free || Values of states or state-action pairs should be estimated and updated according to the temporal changes between subsequent time steps. || Game playing, online learning |

  

## DEEP LEARNING

It is a branch of machine learning that models and resolves complicated issues using multi-layered artificial neural networks or deep neural networks. Deep learning has particularly succeeded in medical image analysis, such as interpreting radiological images and pathology slides.

### Deep Learning Algorithms

“Deep learning” describes a family of machine learning techniques that use multiple-layered neural networks, or “deep neural networks,” to represent and resolve challenging problems. Deep learning has shown impressive results in several fields, such as natural language processing, computer vision, and healthcare. The following are important deep learning algorithms:

#### Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN)

A kind of deep neural networks called Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs or ConvNets) is intended for analysing and understanding visual data. In applications like object identification, picture segmentation, and image classification, CNNs have shown to be incredibly efficient. They excel at jobs where spatial relationships are crucial because of their capacity to automatically extract hierarchical features from input data. Here are key concepts and components of Convolutional Neural Networks:

##### Application

Medical image analysis, pathology detection.

##### Use Case

Analyzing radiological images (X-rays, CT scans) for disease detection.

#### Recurrent Neural Networks (RNN)

A class of neural networks called recurrent neural networks (RNNs) is made to process data sequentially. RNNs can preserve a hidden state that records details about earlier inputs in the sequence because they have connections that form directed cycles, which distinguishes them from typical feedforward neural networks. This makes them ideal for sequence-related applications like speech recognition, time series analysis, and natural language processing. The following are essential ideas and elements of recurrent neural networks:

##### Application

Time-series data analysis, patient monitoring.

##### Use Case

Analyzing continuous patient data, such as vital signs, for early detection of health deterioration.

Table 3 **Pros and Cons of different machine learning models.**  

| **Model** || **Task/Application** || **Pros** || **Cons** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Logistic Regression** || Disease prediction || Simple interpretation, low complexity || Limited in handling complex relationships |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Decision Trees** || Diagnosis, Risk Prediction || Easy to understand, handles non-linearity || Prone to overfitting, instability |
| **Random Forest** || Disease classification || Robust, handles high-dimensional data || Complexity, harder to interpret |
| **Support Vector Machines** || Disease classification || Effective in high-dimensional spaces || Can be sensitive to parameter tuning |
| **Neural Networks** || Image analysis, Diagnosis || Excellent for complex tasks, feature learning || Requires large amounts of data, computationally intensive |
| **Gradient Boosting** || Predictive modeling || High accuracy, handles mixed data types || Sensitive to outliers, potential for overfitting |
| **Naive Bayes** || Text classification || Simple and computationally efficient || Assumes independence of features |
| **k-Nearest Neighbors** || Disease clustering || No training phase, simple implementation || Sensitive to irrelevant features, high memory usage |
| **Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM)** || Time series analysis || Captures long-term dependencies || Complex architecture, longer training times |

  

## RELATION OF GENERATIVE AI WITH HEALTHCARE

Generative AI, a subset of artificial intelligence that can create content such as text, images, and music, has a significant and evolving relationship with healthcare. Here are several key areas where generative AI intersects with healthcare:

### Medical Imaging and Diagnostics

#### Image Generation and Enhancement

Generative AI can create synthetic medical images to augment training datasets, improving the robustness of diagnostic models.

#### Anomaly Detection

AI models can generate images that highlight potential abnormalities, assisting radiologists in identifying conditions like tumors, fractures, and other pathologies.

#### Segmentation and Reconstruction

AI techniques can help in the reconstruction of 3D models from 2D scans, providing detailed insights for complex surgeries.

### Drug Discovery and Development

#### Molecule Generation

Generative models can propose new molecular structures with desired properties, accelerating the initial stages of drug discovery.

#### Simulation of Drug Interactions

AI can simulate how different drug candidates interact with biological targets, helping in the prediction of efficacy and side effects.

### Personalized Medicine

#### Genomic Data Analysis

Generative AI can analyze vast amounts of genomic data to identify patterns and suggest personalized treatment plans.

#### Predictive Modeling

AI can generate predictions about patient outcomes based on historical data, helping in the formulation of personalized treatment strategies.

### Clinical Decision Support

#### Data Synthesis

Generative AI can synthesize patient records and medical literature to provide clinicians with comprehensive insights.

#### Treatment Recommendations

AI systems can generate recommendations for treatments based on the latest research and patient-specific factors.

### Patient Monitoring and Virtual Health Assistants

#### Chatbots and Virtual Assistants

Generative AI powers chatbots that can interact with patients, answer questions, and provide health advice, improving patient engagement and adherence to treatment plans.

#### Symptom Checking

AI systems can generate differential diagnoses based on patient-reported symptoms, guiding them toward appropriate care.

### Administrative Applications

#### Automated Documentation

AI can generate clinical documentation from structured and unstructured data, reducing the administrative burden on healthcare providers.

#### Revenue Cycle Management

Generative AI can help in coding and billing, ensuring accuracy and compliance with regulatory requirements.

### Medical Education and Training

#### Simulation and Training

AI can generate realistic medical scenarios for training healthcare professionals, enhancing their skills through virtual simulations.

#### Content Creation

AI can create educational materials and resources, tailored to the needs of medical students and professionals.

## ETHICAL AND REGULATORY CONSIDERATIONS

### Bias and Fairness

Ensuring that generative AI models that do not perpetuate biases present in the training data is crucial, particularly in healthcare where disparities can have significant impacts on patient outcomes.

### Privacy and Security

Handling sensitive medical data requires stringent privacy and security measures to prevent unauthorized access and misuse.

### Regulatory Compliance

Generative AI applications in healthcare must comply with regulations such as HIPAA in the United States or GDPR in Europe to protect patient information and ensure ethical use.

Generative AI holds transformative potential for healthcare, offering innovative solutions to improve patient outcomes, streamline operations, and foster personalized medicine. However, careful consideration of ethical, legal, and practical aspects is essential to fully realize its benefits.

## LARGE LANGUAGE MODELS (LLMS)

Large Language Models (LLMs) are being increasingly utilized in healthcare to enhance various aspects of patient care, research, and administrative processes. Here are some of the key LLMs used in healthcare and their specific applications:

### GPT Series by OpenAI

GPT models enable the creation of intelligent voice assistants with advanced conversational AI capabilities. Unlike basic chatbots that respond to simple prompts, GPT-powered chatbots can engage in human-like conversations, especially when integrated with other AI technologies. While GPT models are a form of artificial intelligence (AI), they are more precisely neural network-based language models built on Transformer architecture. These models process natural language prompts and generate responses by predicting the most suitable answer. The Transformer architecture utilizes self-attention mechanisms, allowing the models to focus on different parts of the input text, enhancing their understanding and response accuracy.

#### GPT-4

OpenAI's multimodal big language model, known as Generative Pre-trained Transformer 4 (GPT-4), is the fourth model in its GPT foundation model series. It was released to the public on March 14, 2023, using the Microsoft Copilot free chatbot, OpenAI's API, and the premium chatbot ChatGPT Plus. GPT-4 is a transformer-based model that predicts the next token using pre-training on "data licensed from third-party providers" as well as publicly available data.

##### Applications of GPT -4

###### Clinical Documentation

Automating the transcription and summarization of clinical notes.

###### Patient Interaction

Powering chatbots and virtual assistants for patient support.

###### Decision Support

Providing evidence-based recommendations to clinicians.

###### Research Summarization

Assisting in the review and synthesis of medical literature.

#### GPT-3

The third-generation Generative Pre-trained Transformer, or GPT-3, is a machine learning model for neural networks that was taught to produce any kind of text using internet data. It was created by OpenAI, and it only needs a minimal bit of text input to produce enormous amounts of intelligent, pertinent machine-generated content. A deep learning neural network with more than 175 billion machine learning parameters is the model used in GPT-3. To put things in perspective, Microsoft's Turing Natural Language Generation (NLG) model, which included 10 billion parameters, was the largest trained language model prior to GPT-3. GPT-3 is the biggest neural network ever created as of early 2021. Consequently, GPT-3 outperforms all previous models in generating language that is compelling enough to pass for human-written.

##### Applications of GPT -3

###### Symptom Checkers

Guiding patients through self-diagnosis processes.

###### Health Education

Generating educational content for patients and medical professionals.

###### Data Extraction

Analyzing and extracting information from EHRs.

### BERT by Google

#### BERT by Google BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers)

BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) is a state-of- the-art language representation model developed by Google. Introduced in 2018, it has had a profound impact on natural language processing (NLP) tasks by significantly improving how machines understand and generate human language.

##### Applications

###### Natural Language Understanding

Improving the accuracy of natural language understanding in clinical applications.

###### Text Classification

Classifying medical documents and patient records.

###### Information Retrieval

Enhancing the retrieval of relevant information from medical databases.

### T5 by Google

T5 (Text-to-Text Transfer Transformer) is a powerful language model developed by Google Research, introduced in 2019. It is built on the Transformer architecture and designed to unify various natural language processing (NLP) tasks under a single framework.

#### T5 by Google T5 (Text-To-Text Transfer Transformer)

T5 (Text-To-Text Transfer Transformer) is an advanced language model developed by Google Research, introduced in 2019. Its core innovation lies in treating all natural language processing (NLP) tasks as a text-to-text problem, meaning that both the input and output of the model are always text, regardless of the specific task. The model is based on the transformer architecture and is pretrained on a diverse corpus, including the Colossal Clean Crawled Corpus (C4). T5 has different versions, ranging from small to large models, allowing it to balance speed and performance. Fine-tuning T5 for specific tasks leads to state-of- the-art results across many NLP benchmarks.

##### Applications

###### Summarization

Summarizing lengthy medical documents and patient histories.

###### Translation

Translating medical information into multiple languages.

###### Question Answering

Providing accurate answers to medical queries.

### RoBERTa by Facebook AI

RoBERTa (Robustly Optimized BERT Pretraining Approach) is an advanced language model developed by Facebook AI in 2019. It is an improvement over Google’s BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers), and its main objective is to optimize the pretraining procedure of BERT to achieve better performance on natural language processing (NLP) tasks.

#### RoBERTa by Facebook AI RoBERTa (Robustly Optimized BERT Approach)

RoBERTa (Robustly Optimized BERT Approach) is a refined version of Google's BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers), developed by Facebook AI in 2019. RoBERTa aims to improve BERT’s performance by making crucial optimizations in the pretraining process, proving that small adjustments can lead to substantial gains in natural language processing (NLP) tasks.

##### Applications

###### Clinical Text Analysis

Analyzing clinical notes and extracting relevant information.

###### Medical Coding

Assisting in accurate medical coding and billing.

###### Sentiment Analysis

Understanding patient sentiment from feedback and social media.

### XLNet by Google/CMU

XLNet is a cutting-edge language model developed by researchers from Google and Carnegie Mellon University (CMU) in 2019. XLNet was created to address some of the limitations of BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers), while also incorporating advantages from autoregressive models like GPT.

#### XLNet

XLNet achieves state-of-the-art performance in various natural language processing (NLP) tasks by introducing a more sophisticated pretraining mechanism.

##### Applications

###### Predictive Analytics

Predicting patient outcomes based on historical data.

###### Document Classification

Classifying medical documents for research and administration.

###### Contextual Understanding

Enhancing the understanding of complex medical texts.

### BioBERT by Naver

BioBERT is a specialized language model developed by NAVER, designed specifically for biomedical text mining tasks. BioBERT is based on BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers), with a focus on improving performance in the biomedical and healthcare domains.

#### BioBERT (Biomedical BERT)

BioBERT enhances the model’s understanding of biomedical terminology and improves its ability to handle domain-specific tasks like medical question answering, named entity recognition, and relation extraction.

##### Applications

###### Biomedical Text Mining

Extracting information from biomedical literature.

###### Named Entity Recognition

Identifying biomedical entities such as diseases and genes.

###### Relation Extraction

Understanding relationships between biomedical concepts.

### Clinical BERT by MIT

Clinical BERT is a specialized adaptation of the BERT model, developed by researchers at MIT (Massachusetts Institute of Technology) for processing clinical text and electronic health records (EHRs). Its primary goal is to improve the model’s performance on clinical NLP tasks by focusing on the specific language, terminology, and context found in healthcare data. By training on large-scale clinical corpora, Clinical BERT is particularly adept at understanding and extracting meaningful insights from clinical notes and medical documents.

#### Clinical BERT

Clinical BERT is based on the original BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) architecture. Clinical BERT is pretrained on clinical datasets, particularly focusing on MIMIC-III (Medical Information Mart for Intensive Care) — a large, publicly available database of de-identified health records. Clinical BERT achieves state-of-the-art results on several clinical benchmarks.

##### Applications

###### EHR Analysis

Analyzing electronic health records for patient monitoring and decision support.

###### Risk Prediction

Predicting risks such as hospital readmission or disease progression.

###### Patient Outcome Prediction

Improving predictions related to patient outcomes.

### Med-BERT by Microsoft

Med-BERT is a specialized language model developed by Microsoft to improve the understanding and analysis of electronic health records (EHRs). Built upon the popular BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) architecture, Med-BERT is designed to handle structured and unstructured clinical data, with a particular focus on enhancing performance in healthcare-related natural language processing (NLP) tasks. The model’s primary goal is to assist in tasks such as disease prediction, patient outcome forecasting, and clinical document analysis.

#### Med-BERT

Med-BERT is pretrained on **large-scale EHR data** to adapt to the complexities of medical language and terminology. Med-BERT leverages the **BERT** architecture, utilizing **bidirectional training** to capture the context of words from both the left and right sides of a sentence. Med-BERT’s strength lies in its pretraining on vast amounts of de-identified EHR data.

##### Applications

###### Clinical Data Processing

Enhancing the processing of clinical data from EHRs.

###### Patient Trajectory Prediction

Predicting the trajectory of patient health outcomes.

###### Treatment Recommendation

Recommending personalized treatment.

## GENERATIVE ADVERSARIAL NETWORKS (GANS)

Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) are a class of machine learning frameworks designed by Ian Goodfellow and his colleagues in 2014. GANs consist of two neural networks, the generator and the discriminator, which are trained simultaneously through adversarial processes. Here is an overview of GANs, their structure, working mechanism, applications, especially in healthcare, and their challenges.

### Structure of GANs

Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) are a class of machine learning models introduced by Ian Goodfellow and his colleagues in 2014. GANs are designed for generative tasks, where the goal is to produce new data samples that resemble a given training dataset, such as generating realistic images, text, or even audio. The structure of GANs involves two primary neural networks that compete with each other in a zero-sum game: the generator and the discriminator.

#### Generator Network

The generator creates synthetic data samples (*e.g*., images, text) from random noise. Its goal is to produce data that is indistinguishable from real data.

#### Discriminator Network

The discriminator evaluates the data samples and attempts to differentiate between real (from the training set) and fake (generated) samples. Its goal is to correctly identify real *vs*. fake data.

### Working Mechanism

The two networks are trained in a competitive manner:

The **generator** tries to fool the discriminator by producing increasingly realistic samples.

The **discriminator** tries to become better at distinguishing between real and fake samples.

The training process continues until the discriminator can no longer reliably tell the difference between real and generated data, indicating that the generator is producing high-quality samples.

### Applications in Healthcare

#### Medical Image Synthesis

##### Augmenting Datasets

Generating synthetic medical images (*e.g*., MRI, CT scans) to augment training datasets, particularly useful in situations with limited data.

##### Anomaly Detection

Creating realistic images of pathological conditions to train diagnostic models.

#### Image-to-Image Translation

##### Modality Conversion

Translating images from one modality to another (*e.g.*, MRI to CT scans).

##### Enhancement and Restoration

Improving the quality of medical images (*e.g*., removing noise, enhancing resolution).

#### Drug Discovery

##### Molecule Generation

Designing new molecular structures with desired properties, accelerating drug discovery and development.

#### Personalized Medicine

##### Patient-Specific Models

Generating patient-specific data to model disease progression and treatment responses.

#### Genomics

##### Data Simulation

Creating synthetic genomic sequences to test algorithms and tools without compromising patient privacy.

#### Healthcare Training

##### Simulation and Training

Generating realistic medical scenarios for training healthcare professionals, and enhancing their diagnostic and procedural skills.

## RECENT PROGRESS IN THE DEPLOYMENT OF ML ALGORITHMS IN MEDICINE

Studies on medical machine learning frequently follow a well-known pattern: they start with an image classification task, train the machine learning system using supervised learning on labelled data, and then assess the system by comparing it to human experts. Even though these studies have made significant progress, we provide additional exciting research directions that deviate from this pattern. We start by discussing machine learning algorithms, which might offer valuable medical insights. Second, we discuss constraints beyond supervised learning, using paradigms like unsupervised or semi-supervised learning to extract insights from unlabeled or otherwise defective data. In conclusion, we examine machine learning systems that work with humans and some applications, as this represents a way to outperform ML or humans on their own \[[23](#9789815305128-C14.html#r23), [24](#9789815305128-C14.html#r24)\].

Few AI-based ML tools have been implemented in medical practice, even though ML algorithms have been repeatedly demonstrated to be successful in a wide range of retrospective medical studies \[[25](#9789815305128-C14.html#r25)\]. Critics point out that ML algorithms might not be as beneficial in practice as retrospective data would suggest \[[26](#9789815305128-C14.html#r26)\], that they might be too sluggish or complex to be helpful in actual medical settings \[[27](#9789815305128-C14.html#r27)\], or that unanticipated problems might occur from the interactions between humans and AIs \[[28](#9789815305128-C14.html#r28)\]. Prospective research and randomised controlled trials can fill this gap between theory and practice, showing that ML models can be used in healthcare settings and have measurable, beneficial effects.

They recently examined the applicability of ML systems in healthcare. A comprehensive view of AI's effect on medical systems is provided by the numerous metrics employed in addition to accuracy to evaluate the technology's usefulness \[[29](#9789815305128-C14.html#r29)^(\-)[33](#9789815305128-C14.html#r33)\]. For instance, the time patients go within the expected glucose range was measured using an ML-based AI system to manage insulin doses \[[34](#9789815305128-C14.html#r34)\]. The average duration of hypo-tension episodes was tracked in a study monitoring system for preoperative hypotension \[[35](#9789815305128-C14.html#r35)\]. The turnaround time reduction of a system-flagged case of intracranial hemorrhage for human review was assessed \[[36](#9789815305128-C14.html#r36)\].

The community will find it easier to share outcomes and thoroughly exploit thebenefits of medical AI with recent guidelines, like the AI extensions to SPIRIT with CONSORT review, and guidelines, with clinical trial protocols and results \[[37](#9789815305128-C14.html#r37), [38](#9789815305128-C14.html#r38)\].

Certain ML systems advanced from test to deployment in the recent year, and have gained administrative support, navigating legal obstacles. The authorities have approved payments for using two AI systems for image diagnosis, which has aided in adopting AI for clinical setting \[[39](#9789815305128-C14.html#r39)\]. Additionally, a 2020 study has shown that US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has approved AI goods at an accelerated rate, with machine learning (ML) being one sort of AI \[[40](#9789815305128-C14.html#r40)\]. Most of the advancements come in the form of FDA clearances, which have fewer stringent requirements than complete approvals but pave the way for applying AI/ML systems in actual clinical situations. It is crucial to note that the information utilized for these regulatory clearances frequently consists of single-institution, retrospective data deemed proprietary and primarily unpublished. Stronger guidelines for reporting openness and validation, including proof of the systems' influence on clinical output, will be necessary to increase confidence in AI systems \[[41](#9789815305128-C14.html#r41)\].

## FUTURE SCOPE

The future scope of machine learning for the healthcare area is extremely promising and has the potential to change the sector completely. The following are some important areas of healthcare where the use of machine learning technologies is anticipated to be beneficial. By helping pathologists analyse tissue samples and spot irregularities, machine learning can increase the accuracy of diagnoses.

Machine learning can improve medical imaging interpretation speed and accuracy, aiding early detection of diseases like cancer. MRI, X-ray, and CT scan images can all be analyzed by ML algorithms to find patterns that may indicate an illness. ML algorithms can analyse large datasets to find promising drug candidates, estimate their efficacy, and streamline the drug development process.

## CONCLUSION

Machine learning can bring about several technological revolutions in the healthcare industry. It can help identify patterns and trends in patient data, improve diagnosis accuracy, streamline administrative processes, and allow customized treatment plans. However, there are obstacles before machine learning can be applied in the healthcare sector. These include issues with data protection, moral challenges, and the requirement for strict regulation and validation. In summary, an in-depth knowledge of the detailed and constantly changing healthcare environment, cooperation between medical professionals and data scientists, and a commitment to applying machine learning morally and sensibly for patient benefit are all necessary to integrate machine learning in healthcare effectively. Furthermore, machine learning is still not as common in the healthcare sector as it is in other industries because of the limited availability of data and the complexity of medicine.

The requirements to address unique needs and mistrust of the ML system's outputs are two obstacles that make the adoption of ML in healthcare still difficult. Nonetheless, patients have already benefited greatly from the application of ML in healthcare. ML can benefit patients, payers, researchers, and physicians by streamlining workflow and performance, helping medical and non-medical personnel with repetitive activities, helping users discover quick answers to problems, and generating innovative medications.
